# Why Sydney Property Managers Are Investing in Parking Automation

Parking rarely receives much attention when everything is working properly.

When it is not, property managers hear about it quickly.

Unauthorised vehicles take allocated bays. Tenants share access cards. Visitors cannot get through the gate. Staff spend time updating permits and investigating complaints. In properties with casual or guest parking, there is also the question of whether the car park is generating the revenue it could.

For Sydney property managers, these issues are becoming harder to manage with systems built around physical tickets, swipe cards and manual processes.

That is why parking automation is increasingly being viewed as more than an access-control upgrade. Done properly, it can improve how a property is managed, create new revenue opportunities and give operators better control over a valuable part of the asset.

## Key Insight

The business case for parking automation is strongest when it solves a property problem, whether that is creating revenue from previously unmanaged parking, enforcing tenant allocations or reducing day-to-day administration.

## Parking Is Part of the Property Asset

A car park is valuable property.

Yet it is sometimes managed more like a utility than an asset.

For commercial and mixed-use properties, that can mean parking spaces are underutilised, tenant allocations are difficult to enforce or casual parking is available without a clear way to monetise it.

Hotels face a similar issue. Parking may be available for guests, but without a structured system it can remain unmanaged or simply be absorbed into the broader cost of operating the property.

Modern parking automation gives managers more control over how that space is used.

Depending on the site, the system can distinguish between:

- Tenants with allocated bays

- Employees and contractors

- Hotel guests

- Visitors

- Casual paid parkers

- Other authorised vehicles

That allows parking rules to reflect the commercial requirements of the property rather than relying on the same access process for everyone.

## Mercure Brookvale and Macquarie Park: Turning Parking Into Revenue

The Mercure projects at **Brookvale and Macquarie Park** demonstrate the commercial side of parking automation particularly well.

At both sites, parking that had previously been unmanaged or unmonetised became a new revenue stream for the hotels.

The process is straightforward.

Guests are charged a fixed per-night parking rate when they check in, alongside their room. They provide their vehicle number plate, which then becomes their parking credential for the duration of the stay.

Once registered, the guest can use the boom gate without needing a separate parking ticket or physical access card.

For the hotel, parking becomes a defined line item associated with the booking rather than an unmanaged facility sitting outside the guest transaction.

That is an important distinction.

Parking automation is often discussed in terms of saving staff time, and there is an administrative benefit here. But the stronger commercial outcome is that an existing property asset can now generate revenue in a structured way.

The client has confirmed this approach is operating across **two Mercure sites, Brookvale and Macquarie Park**, which also demonstrates that the model can be repeated rather than being specific to a single property.

## ANPR Removes the Need for Separate Parking Credentials

Automatic Number Plate Recognition, or ANPR, allows the vehicle itself to become the access credential.

Instead of giving a tenant, visitor or guest something they need to carry, the system recognises the registration plate as the vehicle approaches.

This can reduce reliance on:

- Swipe cards

- Remotes

- Paper permits

- Parking tickets

- Manual gate access

For property managers, the advantage is not simply convenience.

Vehicle permissions can be managed digitally, and access activity can be recorded by the system. That gives managers greater visibility over who is using the car park and how access is being allocated.

Parkezi's [parking automation systems](https://www.parkezi.com.au/systems) combine ANPR with access control and cloud-based management for sites requiring this level of control.

## Liverpool: When Property Managers Cannot Enforce Tenant Allocations

Hotels provide a useful example of revenue generation, but commercial property managers often face a different problem: ensuring tenants use only the parking they are allocated.

A Parkezi project in Liverpool demonstrates the issue.

The car park serves **more than 30 businesses** and previously operated using swipe cards. The problem was that those cards were being systematically shared.

A business with three allocated bays could share one swipe card between employees and bring significantly more vehicles into the facility. In the example supplied by the client, a tenant with three bays could bring in as many as ten vehicles.

Those vehicles then occupied spaces intended for other businesses.

From the property manager's perspective, the problem was not simply unauthorised access. The people using the cards may have worked for legitimate tenants.

The problem was that the system could not enforce the parking allocation attached to each tenancy.

This is where digital parking management becomes valuable. It gives property managers the ability to manage parking around actual vehicle permissions and allocation rules rather than relying solely on transferable physical credentials.

## Reducing the Everyday Administration Around Parking

Revenue and allocation control are strong reasons to automate, but the administrative benefit should not be overlooked.

Traditional parking management creates numerous small tasks.

A staff member changes cars.

A tenant loses a remote.

A visitor needs temporary access.

A card needs to be cancelled.

A new employee requires parking.

None of these tasks is particularly difficult. The issue is how often they occur.

Across a large property or portfolio, managing physical credentials can become a continuous workload.

Digital parking management moves more of this administration into a central system. Vehicle permissions can be changed without necessarily issuing or recovering another physical item.

For property teams already responsible for building operations, tenants, contractors and maintenance, removing repetitive parking tasks can free up useful time.

## Better Parking Can Improve the Property Experience

Parking is often the first interaction someone has with a property.

A tenant arriving at work, a guest checking into a hotel or a visitor attending a meeting all experience the car park before they reach the building itself.

If access is confusing or unreliable, the property experience starts badly.

The best automated parking experiences are usually uneventful. The authorised vehicle approaches, the system recognises it and the driver continues.

That simplicity is valuable.

For commercial property managers, reliable access can reduce tenant frustration. For hotels, it removes an unnecessary step from the guest journey. For mixed-use sites, different groups can be managed under different rules without requiring every driver to understand what is happening behind the scenes.

## Cloud Management Gives Property Teams Better Visibility

Parking automation also changes what property managers can see.

Instead of relying entirely on physical credentials and manual observations, cloud-based systems can provide visibility over areas such as:

- Vehicle access

- User permissions

- Parking activity

- Occupancy patterns

- System performance

The exact information available depends on how the site is configured, but the principle is important.

A property manager can make better decisions when parking activity is visible rather than assumed.

This becomes particularly useful for multi-site operators, where a central management approach can provide greater consistency across different properties.

## Building the Business Case for Parking Automation

The right business case depends on the problem at the property.

For a hotel, Mercure demonstrates that the opportunity may be **creating a new parking revenue stream**.

For a multi-tenant commercial building, Liverpool demonstrates that the priority may be **enforcing allocations and preventing credential misuse**.

Another property may be primarily concerned with reducing visitor administration or replacing ageing parking hardware.

Before investing, property managers should therefore ask:

- Is parking currently generating the revenue it should?

- Can tenant allocations actually be enforced?

- How much staff time is spent managing credentials?

- Are unauthorised vehicles creating disputes?

- Is the current system scalable as the property changes?

- Does parking create unnecessary friction for tenants or visitors?

The answers help determine whether automation is solving a real commercial problem rather than simply replacing one piece of technology with another.

## Key Takeaways

- Parking should be treated as part of the property asset, not simply an access point.

- Parking automation can create revenue as well as reduce administration.

- At Mercure Brookvale and Macquarie Park, a fixed per-night parking charge is collected at check-in and the guest's number plate provides access for the stay.

- ANPR reduces reliance on transferable physical credentials.

- The Liverpool project shows how swipe-card sharing can make tenant parking allocations difficult to enforce.

- Cloud-based management provides property teams with greater visibility over parking activity.

- The strongest business case starts with the property's actual operational or commercial problem.

## Related Reading

For a closer look at selecting access technology, read [**How to Choose the Right Parking Access Control System for Your Site**](https://www.parkezi.com.au/post/choose-the-right-parking-access-control-system-for-your-site).

You can also explore Parkezi's [parking automation systems](https://www.parkezi.com.au/systems) or learn what is involved in [parking system installation](https://www.parkezi.com.au/installation).

## Frequently Asked Questions

### Can parking automation create additional revenue for a property?

Yes. Where parking is currently unmanaged or unmonetised, automation can provide the structure needed to introduce paid parking. At Mercure Brookvale and Macquarie Park, parking is charged at a fixed nightly rate alongside the guest's room.

### Is ANPR only useful for paid public parking?

No. ANPR can also be used for tenant access, staff parking, hotel guests and other authorised vehicle groups. The value comes from linking parking permissions directly to vehicles.

### Can parking automation help enforce tenant bay allocations?

Yes. Digital access and quota rules can provide more control than transferable swipe cards or remotes, particularly in multi-tenant commercial properties.

## Making Parking Work Harder for the Property

Parking automation should not be introduced simply because the technology is newer.

The question for property managers is whether the existing car park is performing as well as it could.

At the Mercure sites, the opportunity was turning previously unmanaged parking into a structured revenue stream. At Liverpool, the problem was enforcing parking allocations across more than 30 businesses. Both required different outcomes from the parking system.

That is why the right starting point is the property problem, not the product.

If you are reviewing parking across a Sydney commercial, residential, hospitality or mixed-use property, [contact Parkezi](https://www.parkezi.com.au/contact) to discuss where automation could improve revenue, access control or day-to-day management.

Parking for Sydney properties
Parking for Sydney properties
9 September 2026
Parking rarely receives much attention when everything is working properly.
When it is not, property managers hear about it quickly.
Unauthorised vehicles take allocated bays. Tenants share access cards. Visitors cannot get through the gate. Staff spend time updating permits and investigating complaints. In properties with casual or guest parking, there is also the question of whether the car park is generating the revenue it could.
For Sydney property managers, these issues are becoming harder to manage with systems built around physical tickets, swipe cards and manual processes.
That is why parking automation is increasingly being viewed as more than an access-control upgrade. Done properly, it can improve how a property is managed, create new revenue opportunities and give operators better control over a valuable part of the asset.
Key Insight
The business case for parking automation is strongest when it solves a property problem, whether that is creating revenue from previously unmanaged parking, enforcing tenant allocations or reducing day-to-day administration.
Parking Is Part of the Property Asset
A car park is valuable property.
Yet it is sometimes managed more like a utility than an asset.
For commercial and mixed-use properties, that can mean parking spaces are underutilised, tenant allocations are difficult to enforce or casual parking is available without a clear way to monetise it.
Hotels face a similar issue. Parking may be available for guests, but without a structured system it can remain unmanaged or simply be absorbed into the broader cost of operating the property.
Modern parking automation gives managers more control over how that space is used.
Depending on the site, the system can distinguish between:
Tenants with allocated bays
Employees and contractors
Hotel guests
Visitors
Casual paid parkers
Other authorised vehicles
That allows parking rules to reflect the commercial requirements of the property rather than relying on the same access process for everyone.
Mercure: parking revenue
The Mercure projects at
Brookvale and Macquarie Park
demonstrate the commercial side of parking automation particularly well.
At both sites, parking that had previously been unmanaged or unmonetised became a new revenue stream for the hotels.
The process is straightforward.
Guests are charged a fixed per-night parking rate when they check in, alongside their room. They provide their vehicle number plate, which then becomes their parking credential for the duration of the stay.
Once registered, the guest can use the boom gate without needing a separate parking ticket or physical access card.
For the hotel, parking becomes a defined line item associated with the booking rather than an unmanaged facility sitting outside the guest transaction.
That is an important distinction.
Parking automation is often discussed in terms of saving staff time, and there is an administrative benefit here. But the stronger commercial outcome is that an existing property asset can now generate revenue in a structured way.
The client has confirmed this approach is operating across
two Mercure sites, Brookvale and Macquarie Park
, which also demonstrates that the model can be repeated rather than being specific to a single property.
Number plates as credentials
Automatic Number Plate Recognition, or ANPR, allows the vehicle itself to become the access credential.
Instead of giving a tenant, visitor or guest something they need to carry, the system recognises the registration plate as the vehicle approaches.
This can reduce reliance on:
Swipe cards
Remotes
Paper permits
Parking tickets
Manual gate access
For property managers, the advantage is not simply convenience.
Vehicle permissions can be managed digitally, and access activity can be recorded by the system. That gives managers greater visibility over who is using the car park and how access is being allocated.
Parkezi's
parking automation systems
combine ANPR with access control and cloud-based management for sites requiring this level of control.
Liverpool: tenant allocations
Hotels provide a useful example of revenue generation, but commercial property managers often face a different problem: ensuring tenants use only the parking they are allocated.
A Parkezi project in Liverpool demonstrates the issue.
The car park serves
more than 30 businesses
and previously operated using swipe cards. The problem was that those cards were being systematically shared.
A business with three allocated bays could share one swipe card between employees and bring significantly more vehicles into the facility. In the example supplied by the client, a tenant with three bays could bring in as many as ten vehicles.
Those vehicles then occupied spaces intended for other businesses.
From the property manager's perspective, the problem was not simply unauthorised access. The people using the cards may have worked for legitimate tenants.
The problem was that the system could not enforce the parking allocation attached to each tenancy.
This is where digital parking management becomes valuable. It gives property managers the ability to manage parking around actual vehicle permissions and allocation rules rather than relying solely on transferable physical credentials.
Less parking administration
Revenue and allocation control are strong reasons to automate, but the administrative benefit should not be overlooked.
Traditional parking management creates numerous small tasks.
A staff member changes cars.
A tenant loses a remote.
A visitor needs temporary access.
A card needs to be cancelled.
A new employee requires parking.
None of these tasks is particularly difficult. The issue is how often they occur.
Across a large property or portfolio, managing physical credentials can become a continuous workload.
Digital parking management moves more of this administration into a central system. Vehicle permissions can be changed without necessarily issuing or recovering another physical item.
For property teams already responsible for building operations, tenants, contractors and maintenance, removing repetitive parking tasks can free up useful time.
The property experience
Parking is often the first interaction someone has with a property.
A tenant arriving at work, a guest checking into a hotel or a visitor attending a meeting all experience the car park before they reach the building itself.
If access is confusing or unreliable, the property experience starts badly.
The best automated parking experiences are usually uneventful. The authorised vehicle approaches, the system recognises it and the driver continues.
That simplicity is valuable.
For commercial property managers, reliable access can reduce tenant frustration. For hotels, it removes an unnecessary step from the guest journey. For mixed-use sites, different groups can be managed under different rules without requiring every driver to understand what is happening behind the scenes.
Cloud visibility for property teams
Parking automation also changes what property managers can see.
Instead of relying entirely on physical credentials and manual observations, cloud-based systems can provide visibility over areas such as:
Vehicle access
User permissions
Parking activity
Occupancy patterns
System performance
The exact information available depends on how the site is configured, but the principle is important.
A property manager can make better decisions when parking activity is visible rather than assumed.
This becomes particularly useful for multi-site operators, where a central management approach can provide greater consistency across different properties.
The business case
The right business case depends on the problem at the property.
For a hotel, Mercure demonstrates that the opportunity may be
creating a new parking revenue stream
.
For a multi-tenant commercial building, Liverpool demonstrates that the priority may be
enforcing allocations and preventing credential misuse
.
Another property may be primarily concerned with reducing visitor administration or replacing ageing parking hardware.
Before investing, property managers should therefore ask:
Is parking currently generating the revenue it should?
Can tenant allocations actually be enforced?
How much staff time is spent managing credentials?
Are unauthorised vehicles creating disputes?
Is the current system scalable as the property changes?
Does parking create unnecessary friction for tenants or visitors?
The answers help determine whether automation is solving a real commercial problem rather than simply replacing one piece of technology with another.
Key Takeaways
Parking should be treated as part of the property asset, not simply an access point.
Parking automation can create revenue as well as reduce administration.
At Mercure Brookvale and Macquarie Park, a fixed per-night parking charge is collected at check-in and the guest's number plate provides access for the stay.
ANPR reduces reliance on transferable physical credentials.
The Liverpool project shows how swipe-card sharing can make tenant parking allocations difficult to enforce.
Cloud-based management provides property teams with greater visibility over parking activity.
The strongest business case starts with the property's actual operational or commercial problem.
Related Reading
For a closer look at selecting access technology, read
How to Choose the Right Parking Access Control System for Your Site
.
You can also explore Parkezi's
parking automation systems
or learn what is involved in
parking system installation
.
Questions answered
Can it increase parking revenue?
Yes. Where parking is currently unmanaged or unmonetised, automation can provide the structure needed to introduce paid parking. At Mercure Brookvale and Macquarie Park, parking is charged at a fixed nightly rate alongside the guest's room.
Is ANPR only useful for paid public parking?
No. ANPR can also be used for tenant access, staff parking, hotel guests and other authorised vehicle groups. The value comes from linking parking permissions directly to vehicles.
Can it enforce tenant allocations?
Yes. Digital access and quota rules can provide more control than transferable swipe cards or remotes, particularly in multi-tenant commercial properties.
Making Parking Work Harder for the Property
Parking automation should not be introduced simply because the technology is newer.
The question for property managers is whether the existing car park is performing as well as it could.
At the Mercure sites, the opportunity was turning previously unmanaged parking into a structured revenue stream. At Liverpool, the problem was enforcing parking allocations across more than 30 businesses. Both required different outcomes from the parking system.
That is why the right starting point is the property problem, not the product.

contact Parkezi
to discuss where automation could improve revenue, access control or day-to-day management.

post/choose-the-right-parking-access-control-system-for-your-site article

Source body/lead retained with current links and shorter headings.

Original source · Redesigned route

Before: captured original

# How to Choose the Right Parking Access Control System for Your Site

Choosing a parking access control system can look straightforward until you start comparing what different sites actually need.

A small residential building with 20 regular vehicles has very different requirements from a commercial property shared by dozens of businesses. A hotel needs to manage guests differently from staff, while a high-security facility may be more concerned about unauthorised vehicles and tailgating than payment.

That is why choosing the right system should start with the site, not the technology.

Swipe cards, fobs, ANPR cameras, boom gates and cloud-based management platforms all have their place. The challenge is working out which combination provides the right balance of security, convenience and long-term management for your property.

## Key Insight

The best parking access control system is not automatically the most advanced. It is the one that solves your site's specific access and security problems without creating unnecessary complexity.

## Start With How Your Car Park Is Actually Used

Before comparing products, understand who needs access and how frequently they use the facility.

Consider:

- How many vehicles enter each day?

- What happens during peak periods?

- Are users permanent tenants, staff, visitors or casual parkers?

- Do different users have different access rights?

- Are parking bays allocated to individual tenants?

- Does the site operate outside staffed hours?

- How serious are the consequences of unauthorised access?

These questions help define what the system actually needs to do.

For example, a residential property with a small, stable group of users may not need the same technology as a multi-tenant commercial building where hundreds of vehicles and changing access permissions need to be managed.

## Cards and Fobs Versus ANPR

Traditional parking access systems typically use swipe cards, fobs, remotes or keypads.

They are familiar and can still be perfectly practical in the right environment.

The main limitation is that the credential belongs to whoever is holding it.

A card can be shared. A remote can be passed between vehicles. A lost credential can remain active until somebody notices and disables it.

ANPR takes a different approach.

Instead of asking the driver to present something, the system recognises the vehicle's number plate. For authorised vehicles, access can happen automatically.

This also creates a digital record of vehicle movements and allows access permissions to be changed without collecting physical credentials.

The difference becomes particularly important when parking allocations need to be enforced.

## Liverpool: What Happens When Access Cards Are Shared

A Parkezi project in Liverpool demonstrates why choosing the access method matters.

The car park serves **more than 30 businesses** and previously operated using swipe cards.

The problem was that cards were being systematically shared. A business allocated three parking bays could pass one card between drivers and bring in as many as ten vehicles.

Those additional vehicles then occupied spaces allocated to other tenants.

There was no quota system to prevent it and no practical way to enforce how many vehicles each business had on site.

This is an important distinction when evaluating access control.

Providing access and **controlling access** are not necessarily the same thing.

A credential may successfully open a door or gate, but that does not mean the system is enforcing how the parking facility is supposed to be used.

For multi-tenant properties, the ability to manage vehicle permissions and allocations can therefore be just as important as the physical barrier itself.

## Tailgating Requires More Than Vehicle Recognition

Another issue at the Liverpool site was physical security.

There were no boom gates. The car park relied on a roller shutter, which was relatively slow to close. Once the shutter opened for an authorised vehicle, another vehicle could potentially follow it inside before it closed.

Parkezi addressed this using an interlocked boom gate and roller shutter system.

Both barriers open together for an authorised vehicle. The **Beninca DIVA.3 boom gate** then drops rapidly behind the vehicle, helping prevent another car from tailgating through the open entrance. The roller shutter subsequently closes to secure the car park.

This highlights something that can be missed when comparing access systems.

ANPR can determine whether a vehicle should be authorised, but the physical access-control design determines what happens immediately afterwards.

If tailgating is a genuine security risk, camera recognition alone is not enough. Barrier speed, positioning, vehicle detection and the sequence between different physical controls all need to be considered.

## When ANPR Is Not the Right Fit

ANPR has significant advantages, but a genuine buyer's guide also needs to acknowledge its limitations.

Number plate recognition depends on the camera having a readable view of the plate. Damaged, heavily obscured or otherwise unreadable plates can affect recognition.

Site layout and camera positioning also matter. A poorly designed installation can undermine otherwise capable technology.

And some sites simply do not need ANPR.

For a small, low-turnover residential car park with a stable group of users and straightforward access requirements, an existing card or fob system may continue to provide everything the property needs.

The decision should come back to the problem being solved.

ANPR becomes particularly valuable when you need better vehicle-level accountability, remote access management, automated entry or more sophisticated control across larger or changing user groups.

## Think About Privacy Before Installation

ANPR introduces another consideration that should be addressed during procurement: privacy.

Number plates linked to identifiable individuals can constitute personal information, which means organisations need to think carefully about how that information is collected and managed. The client has specifically identified this as one of the first questions strata committees and property managers raise when evaluating ANPR.

Before selecting a provider, ask:

- Where is number plate data stored?

- Who can access it?

- What security controls protect that information?

- How long are vehicle records retained?

- What happens when information is no longer required?

- How are users informed about the collection and use of their data?

These questions should form part of the buying process rather than being addressed after installation.

**Parkezi-specific storage, access and retention details should be inserted here once confirmed by Parkezi.**

That is preferable to making broad claims about privacy compliance without explaining what happens to the data in practice.

## Look at the Complete Parking System

Access control rarely operates effectively in isolation.

Depending on the property, it may need to connect with:

- Parking management software

- Payment systems

- Tenant access permissions

- Reporting and analytics

- Boom gates and shutters

- Other property management systems

Integration reduces manual administration and gives operators a clearer view of what is happening across the facility.

A property manager should therefore ask not only whether a system can open the gate, but also whether it fits into the broader way the property is managed.

You can explore how Parkezi combines these components through its [parking automation systems](https://www.parkezi.com.au/systems).

## Consider Long-Term Management, Not Just Purchase Price

Upfront cost matters, but it is only one part of the decision.

Traditional credential systems can carry ongoing administrative costs. Cards and fobs need to be issued, replaced and disabled. Changes to tenants or staff need to be managed, and physical credentials can continue circulating unless there is a reliable process for retrieving them.

Cloud-managed systems shift more of this work into software.

Access permissions can be changed remotely, new vehicles can be added and multiple sites can potentially be managed from a central platform.

The right comparison therefore considers:

- Initial installation

- Ongoing administration

- Hardware maintenance

- Credential replacement

- System scalability

- Security requirements

- Integration requirements

A cheaper system at installation may not remain the cheaper option over its operating life.

## Key Takeaways

- Start with your site's users, traffic patterns and security requirements before comparing technology.

- Cards and fobs remain suitable for some smaller, straightforward sites.

- ANPR provides vehicle-level access control without relying on transferable physical credentials.

- The Liverpool project shows how shared swipe cards can undermine tenant parking allocations.

- Tailgating requires physical security measures as well as vehicle recognition.

- Parkezi's Liverpool solution uses a Beninca DIVA.3 boom gate interlocked with a roller shutter to rapidly secure access behind each vehicle.

- ANPR is not suitable for every site or every vehicle condition.

- Privacy, data storage, access and retention should be considered before an ANPR system is selected.

- Long-term administration and maintenance matter alongside upfront cost.

## Related Reading

For a broader look at the technology available, explore Parkezi's [parking automation systems](https://www.parkezi.com.au/systems).

If you are planning an upgrade, Parkezi also explains what to consider during [parking system installation](https://www.parkezi.com.au/installation).

If revenue performance is part of the reason for reviewing your existing setup, read [How to Improve Parking Revenue Without Increasing Fees](https://www.parkezi.com.au/post/improve-parking-revenue-without-increasing-fees) for a different perspective on how ANPR and ticketless parking can improve car park performance.

## Frequently Asked Questions

### Is ANPR always better than swipe cards or fobs?

No. ANPR is particularly useful where operators need vehicle-level accountability, automated entry and easier management of changing users. A small residential site with a stable user base may still be well served by a simple card or fob system.

### Can ANPR stop tailgating?

ANPR determines whether a vehicle is authorised, but preventing tailgating also depends on the physical access-control design. At Parkezi's Liverpool project, a rapid-closing Beninca DIVA.3 boom gate was interlocked with the roller shutter specifically to address this issue.

### What happens if a number plate cannot be read?

Dirty, damaged or obscured plates can affect recognition, which is why system design should include an appropriate way to handle exceptions. Camera positioning and installation quality also play an important role in recognition reliability.

### What privacy questions should I ask about ANPR?

Ask where vehicle data is stored, who can access it, how it is secured, how long it is retained and what process is used when records are no longer required.

## Finding the Right System for Your Site

There is no single parking access control system that is right for every property.

For some sites, a simple credential system remains entirely appropriate. For others, physical credentials create exactly the management and security problems they are trying to solve.

The Liverpool project is a useful example. The problem was not simply outdated swipe cards. It was that the system could not enforce parking allocations or adequately control the physical entrance once access had been granted.

Solving that required a combination of digital access management and carefully designed physical security.

If you are comparing parking access control options, [contact Parkezi](https://www.parkezi.com.au/contact) to discuss your site's traffic, security and management requirements before deciding which technology is the right fit.

The decision should come back to the problem being solved.
ANPR becomes particularly valuable when you need better vehicle-level accountability, remote access management, automated entry or more sophisticated control across larger or changing user groups.
Think About Privacy Before Installation
ANPR introduces another consideration that should be addressed during procurement: privacy.
Number plates linked to identifiable individuals can constitute personal information, which means organisations need to think carefully about how that information is collected and managed. The client has specifically identified this as one of the first questions strata committees and property managers raise when evaluating ANPR.
Before selecting a provider, ask:
Where is number plate data stored?
Who can access it?
What security controls protect that information?
How long are vehicle records retained?
What happens when information is no longer required?
How are users informed about the collection and use of their data?
These questions should form part of the buying process rather than being addressed after installation.
Parkezi-specific storage, access and retention details should be inserted here once confirmed by Parkezi.
That is preferable to making broad claims about privacy compliance without explaining what happens to the data in practice.
Look at the Complete Parking System
Access control rarely operates effectively in isolation.
Depending on the property, it may need to connect with:
Parking management software
Payment systems
Tenant access permissions
Reporting and analytics
Boom gates and shutters
Other property management systems
Integration reduces manual administration and gives operators a clearer view of what is happening across the facility.
A property manager should therefore ask not only whether a system can open the gate, but also whether it fits into the broader way the property is managed.
You can explore how Parkezi combines these components through its
parking automation systems
.
Long-term management costs
Upfront cost matters, but it is only one part of the decision.
Traditional credential systems can carry ongoing administrative costs. Cards and fobs need to be issued, replaced and disabled. Changes to tenants or staff need to be managed, and physical credentials can continue circulating unless there is a reliable process for retrieving them.
Cloud-managed systems shift more of this work into software.
Access permissions can be changed remotely, new vehicles can be added and multiple sites can potentially be managed from a central platform.
The right comparison therefore considers:
Initial installation
Ongoing administration
Hardware maintenance
Credential replacement
System scalability
Security requirements
Integration requirements
A cheaper system at installation may not remain the cheaper option over its operating life.
Key Takeaways
Start with your site's users, traffic patterns and security requirements before comparing technology.
Cards and fobs remain suitable for some smaller, straightforward sites.
ANPR provides vehicle-level access control without relying on transferable physical credentials.
The Liverpool project shows how shared swipe cards can undermine tenant parking allocations.
Tailgating requires physical security measures as well as vehicle recognition.
Parkezi's Liverpool solution uses a Beninca DIVA.3 boom gate interlocked with a roller shutter to rapidly secure access behind each vehicle.
ANPR is not suitable for every site or every vehicle condition.
Privacy, data storage, access and retention should be considered before an ANPR system is selected.
Long-term administration and maintenance matter alongside upfront cost.
Related Reading
For a broader look at the technology available, explore Parkezi's
parking automation systems
.
If you are planning an upgrade, Parkezi also explains what to consider during
parking system installation
.
If revenue performance is part of the reason for reviewing your existing setup, read
How to Improve Parking Revenue Without Increasing Fees
for a different perspective on how ANPR and ticketless parking can improve car park performance.
Questions answered
ANPR, cards or fobs?
No. ANPR is particularly useful where operators need vehicle-level accountability, automated entry and easier management of changing users. A small residential site with a stable user base may still be well served by a simple card or fob system.
Can ANPR stop tailgating?
ANPR determines whether a vehicle is authorised, but preventing tailgating also depends on the physical access-control design. At Parkezi's Liverpool project, a rapid-closing Beninca DIVA.3 boom gate was interlocked with the roller shutter specifically to address this issue.
What if a plate cannot be read?
Dirty, damaged or obscured plates can affect recognition, which is why system design should include an appropriate way to handle exceptions. Camera positioning and installation quality also play an important role in recognition reliability.
ANPR privacy questions
Ask where vehicle data is stored, who can access it, how it is secured, how long it is retained and what process is used when records are no longer required.
Finding the Right System for Your Site
There is no single parking access control system that is right for every property.
For some sites, a simple credential system remains entirely appropriate. For others, physical credentials create exactly the management and security problems they are trying to solve.
The Liverpool project is a useful example. The problem was not simply outdated swipe cards. It was that the system could not enforce parking allocations or adequately control the physical entrance once access had been granted.
Solving that required a combination of digital access management and carefully designed physical security.
If you are comparing parking access control options,
contact Parkezi
to discuss your site's traffic, security and management requirements before deciding which technology is the right fit.

Discuss access, payments and the setup your car park needs.

# How Ticketless Parking Validation Works for Retail and Hospitality Sites

Parking validation has traditionally involved a surprisingly physical process.

A customer parks, collects a paper ticket, visits a restaurant, hotel or retail business, then asks a staff member to stamp, scan or validate the ticket before returning to the car park.

It works, but it creates extra steps for everyone involved.

Customers need to remember their ticket. Staff need access to the validation process. Lost tickets create another problem to resolve. In a mixed-use property, things become even more complicated when residents, hotel guests, staff and casual visitors all have different parking rules.

Ticketless parking validation takes a different approach.

Instead of relying on a paper ticket, the system uses the vehicle's number plate to identify the parking session. That parking record can then be connected with the customer's transaction, booking or other validation method.

The result is a parking experience that can accommodate different users without asking everyone to follow the same process.

## Key Insight

The real benefit of ticketless parking validation is not simply removing paper tickets. It is allowing different businesses and user groups to apply their own parking rules within the same car park.

## Why Traditional Parking Validation Creates Friction

Parking validation sounds simple until you consider what needs to happen behind the scenes.

A restaurant might offer free parking to customers who spend over a certain amount. A hotel may include parking with selected bookings. Residents may have permanent access, while members of the public need to pay the standard casual rate.

With a ticket-based system, each of these rules needs a process.

Customers may need to:

- Collect and keep a paper ticket

- Take it into the venue

- Ask a staff member for validation

- Return to a payment station

- Insert or scan the validated ticket

- Complete any remaining payment before leaving

For the business, staff need to understand who qualifies, how validation works and what to do when a ticket is lost or the process fails.

Ticketless parking removes much of this physical interaction by linking the parking session to the vehicle instead.

## How Ticketless Validation Works

Automatic Number Plate Recognition, or ANPR, records a vehicle's number plate when it enters the car park.

The vehicle registration becomes the identifier for that parking session.

From there, validation can be applied according to the site's requirements.

Depending on the property, this could involve:

- Hotel reception validating a guest

- A restaurant applying parking based on customer spend

- Residents being automatically recognised

- Staff receiving predefined access

- Casual users paying the standard parking rate

The important difference is that the driver does not need to carry a physical ticket through the process.

The system already knows when the vehicle entered. The validation determines what parking rule should apply when it leaves.

Parkezi's [parking automation systems](https://www.parkezi.com.au/systems) can combine ANPR, access control and payment processes within this type of environment.

## Northies Hotel and Quest Apartments: One Car Park, Different Users

The Northies Hotel and Quest Apartments project in Cronulla provides a strong example of why this matters.

The site needed to accommodate several user groups within the same parking environment, including:

- Quest residents and guests

- Northies Hotel customers

- Staff

- Businesses

- Members of the public

Each group had different requirements.

Trying to manage all of them through the same ticket-based process would create unnecessary complexity.

Instead, the Parkezi system allows parking rules to be applied according to who is using the facility.

Residents and other authorised users can be managed through vehicle registration and access permissions.

Hotel and hospitality customers can have parking validated according to the rules established by the venue.

Public users can still access the facility under the standard paid parking arrangement.

The result is one car park supporting several different parking experiences without requiring separate physical systems for each user group.

## Restaurant POS Integration Changes the Validation Process

One of the most distinctive parts of the Northies project is the integration between parking validation and the restaurant's point-of-sale system.

Traditionally, a customer who qualifies for parking validation might need to present a parking ticket to a staff member and ask for it to be stamped or processed separately.

At Northies, the validation can instead be connected to the restaurant transaction.

The client describes the process as follows: if a customer spends **$50 in the restaurant, they receive two hours of free parking**.

The customer does not need to carry a paper parking ticket.

Their parking session can be validated through the hospitality transaction, allowing the parking benefit to become part of the customer experience rather than a separate process.

That is a meaningful difference for both the customer and the venue.

Staff do not need to manage piles of tickets or operate a disconnected validation process. Customers receive the parking benefit associated with their visit without having to understand the technology behind it.

## Different Businesses Can Apply Different Parking Rules

Mixed-use properties often struggle with parking because different businesses want different outcomes.

A restaurant may want to reward customers with free parking after a qualifying purchase.

A hotel may want to provide parking as part of a guest stay.

A commercial tenant may require allocated staff access.

Residents may need unrestricted entry for registered vehicles.

Casual visitors may simply need a convenient way to pay.

A ticketless system can manage these groups under different rules while still using the same physical car park.

This makes the parking facility more adaptable to the commercial needs of the property.

Rather than forcing every business into one parking model, the system can support the way each part of the site actually operates.

## Why This Matters for Customer Experience

Parking validation should feel like a benefit.

If claiming that benefit requires several additional steps, some of its value is lost.

A restaurant customer who has just finished a meal should not need to remember whether they left their parking ticket in the car.

A hotel guest should not have to understand a complicated payment process simply to leave the property.

Ticketless parking helps move these interactions into the background.

For the customer, the ideal experience is straightforward:

They arrive. They visit the venue. The appropriate parking rule is applied. They leave.

That simplicity is particularly valuable in hospitality, where the parking experience contributes to the overall impression of the venue.

## Ticketless Does Not Always Mean Contactless

It is also useful to distinguish between **ticketless** and **contactless** parking.

The terms are related, but they describe different parts of the experience.

Ticketless parking removes the physical paper ticket.

Contactless parking reduces or removes physical touchpoints during access and payment.

A system can therefore be ticketless while still allowing a driver to pay by card at a terminal. It can also combine ticketless identification with contactless payment methods such as mobile or digital payments.

For retail and hospitality operators, understanding this distinction helps when designing the right customer journey.

The objective should be to remove the interactions that create unnecessary friction rather than simply adopting technology for its own sake.

## Better Control for Operators

Ticketless validation also provides operators with greater visibility.

Instead of relying on physical tickets moving between the car park and individual businesses, parking activity can be managed digitally.

Depending on the system configuration, operators can see information relating to:

- Vehicle entry and exit

- Validation activity

- Different user groups

- Parking permissions

- Payment status

This makes it easier to understand how the car park is being used and whether the validation rules are working as intended.

For mixed-use properties, that visibility is particularly useful because several businesses may depend on the same parking infrastructure.

## Key Takeaways

- Ticketless parking validation uses the vehicle's number plate instead of a paper ticket to identify the parking session.

- Different user groups can have different parking rules within the same facility.

- Northies Hotel and Quest Apartments manage hotel, hospitality, residential, business, staff and public parking within one environment.

- At Northies, customers who spend $50 in the restaurant can receive two hours of free parking through the venue's validation process.

- Integrating parking with hospitality transactions reduces the need for separate paper-ticket validation.

- Ticketless and contactless parking are related but distinct concepts.

- Digital validation can simplify the customer experience while giving operators greater control over parking rules.

## Related Reading

Explore Parkezi's [parking automation systems](https://www.parkezi.com.au/systems) to see how ANPR, payments and access control can work together.

For businesses considering an upgrade from traditional parking equipment, learn more about Parkezi's [parking system installation process](https://www.parkezi.com.au/installation).

You can also explore Parkezi's [completed parking projects](https://www.parkezi.com.au/our-work) to see how different sites have approached parking automation.

## Frequently Asked Questions

### What is ticketless parking validation?

Ticketless validation identifies the parking session through the vehicle's number plate rather than a paper ticket. A hotel, restaurant or other business can then apply the relevant parking benefit digitally.

### Can restaurants offer free parking based on customer spend?

Yes, where the parking and point-of-sale systems are configured to support it. At Northies, the client has confirmed that customers spending $50 in the restaurant can receive two hours of free parking.

### Can one car park have different rules for different businesses?

Yes. A ticketless parking system can apply different access, payment and validation rules to residents, hotel guests, staff, customers and casual parkers within the same facility.

### Is ticketless parking the same as contactless parking?

No. Ticketless means there is no physical paper parking ticket. Contactless refers to reducing physical interaction during access or payment. A parking system can incorporate both.

## Making Parking Validation Part of the Customer Experience

For retail and hospitality businesses, parking should support the customer experience rather than interrupt it.

The Northies Hotel and Quest Apartments project demonstrates what becomes possible when parking is connected with the way the businesses on site actually operate.

Instead of asking every driver to follow the same ticket process, different rules can be applied to different users. Restaurant customers can receive parking benefits through their visit, hotel and residential users can have appropriate access, and casual users can still pay for parking normally.

That is where ticketless parking validation becomes more valuable than simply removing paper.

It allows the parking system to work around the customer journey.

If you manage a hotel, restaurant, retail destination or mixed-use property and want to simplify parking validation, [contact Parkezi](https://www.parkezi.com.au/contact) to discuss how ticketless parking can be configured around your site's customers and operating model.

post/tenant-parking-quota-management-solves-a-common-commercial-property-problem article

Source body/lead retained with current links and shorter headings.

Original source · Redesigned route

Before: captured original

# How Tenant Parking Quota Management Solves a Common Commercial Property Problem

A business leases three parking bays in a commercial building.

That sounds simple enough. Three bays should mean no more than three of that tenant's vehicles are parked on site at any one time.

But what happens if the business has six, eight or ten staff members who drive to work?

With a traditional swipe card or remote system, enforcing that three-bay allocation can become surprisingly difficult. Credentials get shared between staff, vehicles change from day to day and building managers can end up resolving disputes when one tenant's vehicles occupy spaces allocated to someone else.

This is not really an access problem. The vehicles may all belong to legitimate tenants.

It is a **quota management problem**.

For multi-tenant commercial properties, ANPR-based tenant parking management provides a different way to approach it. Instead of controlling which individual vehicles are permanently allowed to enter, the system can control how many vehicles each tenant is allowed to have on site at the same time.

## Key Insight

Effective tenant parking management is not about limiting how many vehicles a business can register. It is about enforcing how many of those registered vehicles can occupy the car park at once.

## Why Traditional Access Systems Struggle With Parking Allocations

Swipe cards, remotes and fobs are designed primarily to answer one question: should the person holding this credential be allowed through the gate?

That works well for basic access control.

It becomes more difficult when the property needs to enforce commercial parking allocations.

Imagine a tenant leases three bays but has ten employees who occasionally drive to work. Giving the business only three swipe cards seems like an obvious solution.

The problem is that cards can be passed between staff.

One employee arrives, enters using the card and passes it to someone else. Before long, the business can have considerably more than three vehicles inside the car park.

The access system has technically done its job. Every entry used a valid credential.

But the parking allocation has failed.

This distinction is important for commercial property managers because it explains why simply upgrading a gate or issuing new cards does not necessarily solve tenant parking disputes.

## Tenant Quotas Change What the System Controls

ANPR-based quota management approaches the problem differently.

Rather than making the physical access credential the centre of the system, vehicle registrations and tenant allocations are managed digitally.

A tenant can register multiple vehicles that may legitimately need parking access.

The quota then determines how many can be parked inside at the same time.

For example, a company leasing three bays might register:

- Two directors' vehicles

- Four employee vehicles

- Two pool vehicles

There is no problem with having eight vehicles registered.

The important rule is that only three can occupy the tenant's allocated parking capacity at once.

When a fourth registered vehicle arrives, the system can identify that the tenant has already reached its quota and apply the appropriate rule.

That is fundamentally different from trying to control parking allocation by restricting the number of cards issued.

## Knight Frank Chatswood: Three Bays Can Still Mean Four Registered Cars

Parkezi's Knight Frank Chatswood project provides a practical example.

The 120-bay property includes **50 leased bays and 70 casual bays**, with **18 tenant companies** using the building. Each tenant company has its own login.

Under the quota management system, tenants can whitelist as many vehicles as they need.

What they cannot do is occupy more bays than they have paid for.

If a tenant has three leased bays and four registered vehicles, any three of those vehicles can be on site.

When the fourth arrives, the system recognises that the tenant's allocation has already been reached. The additional vehicle can then be notified to leave or be charged the casual parking rate.

The property manager does not need to decide which three employees are permanently entitled to park.

There are no physical permits to distribute and no cards for the building manager to continually administer.

The tenant manages its own registered vehicles while the system enforces the actual parking allocation.

That flexibility is particularly useful in modern workplaces where the people coming into an office can change throughout the week.

## Liverpool Shows What Happens Without Quota Enforcement

The Liverpool project demonstrates the same problem from the opposite direction.

The car park serves **more than 30 businesses** and previously relied on swipe cards.

Cards were being systematically shared. A business with three allocated bays could share one card among its staff and bring as many as ten vehicles into the facility.

Those additional vehicles did not simply exceed that tenant's allocation. They occupied parking intended for other businesses.

There was no quota system capable of enforcing how many vehicles each tenant had inside.

This is why parking allocation disputes can be difficult to solve manually.

From the driver's perspective, they may have legitimate access to the building. From the tenant's perspective, the employee may genuinely work there.

But neither fact means the business should be able to occupy more parking bays than it leases.

A parking management system needs to distinguish between **being an authorised user** and **having available parking allocation at that moment**.

## Giving Tenants More Control Can Reduce Building Administration

Parking management often becomes a property manager's problem because the building manager sits between the tenant and the access system.

A new employee starts and needs parking.

Someone changes vehicles.

A registration needs updating.

A card is lost.

An employee leaves and access needs to be removed.

When all of those changes need to go through building management, a relatively simple parking facility can create an ongoing stream of administrative work.

Tenant-level access changes that relationship.

At Knight Frank, each of the 18 tenant companies has its own login.

That allows responsibility for managing authorised vehicles to sit closer to the business using the bays, while the overall parking rules remain controlled by the system.

For property managers, the benefit is not simply automation. It is removing routine parking administration that does not need to sit with the building management team.

## Quota Management Also Makes Parking Fairer

Parking disputes are often difficult because enforcement can feel subjective.

One tenant may complain that another business is occupying too many spaces. Building management then has to investigate who owns the vehicles, whether they are authorised and which tenant should actually be using each bay.

A digitally enforced quota creates a clearer rule.

If a business leases five bays, its simultaneous parking allocation is five vehicles.

It can potentially register more vehicles to accommodate hybrid working, staff changes and different daily schedules, but the physical parking entitlement remains the same.

That makes the system more flexible for tenants without reducing fairness for everyone else in the building.

It also gives property managers a clearer basis for dealing with parking enquiries because access activity is recorded digitally rather than relying entirely on physical permits or observations.

## Where Tenant Quota Management Makes Sense

Not every property needs sophisticated quota management.

A small building with a handful of permanent users and fixed allocated bays may be adequately managed with simpler access control.

Quota management becomes particularly useful where there are:

- Multiple commercial tenants

- Limited parking relative to staff numbers

- Flexible or hybrid working arrangements

- Regular changes in employee vehicles

- Shared commercial parking areas

- Ongoing disputes over parking allocations

- Physical access credentials being shared

The more dynamic the tenant population becomes, the less practical it is to treat a swipe card as a reliable representation of a parking bay.

For properties facing those issues, [ANPR-enabled parking management](https://www.parkezi.com.au/systems) provides a way to connect vehicle access with actual parking entitlement.

## Key Takeaways

- Access permission and parking entitlement are two different things.

- Swipe cards and remotes can be shared, making tenant parking quotas difficult to enforce.

- ANPR allows tenants to register multiple legitimate vehicles without giving them unlimited parking capacity.

- A tenant leasing three bays can register more than three vehicles while still being limited to three vehicles on site at once.

- Knight Frank Chatswood uses tenant logins and quota management across 18 tenant companies.

- At Liverpool, a lack of quota enforcement allowed businesses to significantly exceed their allocations by sharing swipe cards.

- Tenant self-management can reduce routine administration for property managers.

- Quota-based rules create clearer and fairer parking allocation across multi-tenant properties.

## Related Reading

For a broader comparison of different access technologies, read[**How to Choose the Right Parking Access Control System for Your Site**](https://www.parkezi.com.au/post/choose-the-right-parking-access-control-system-for-your-site).

You can also explore Parkezi's [parking automation systems](https://www.parkezi.com.au/systems) to understand how ANPR, access control and cloud management work together.

If you are considering replacing an existing swipe-card or permit system, Parkezi's [parking system installation process](https://www.parkezi.com.au/installation) explains what is involved in planning an upgrade.

## Frequently Asked Questions

### Can a tenant register more vehicles than the number of bays it leases?

Yes. That is one of the advantages of quota-based management. A tenant can have multiple authorised vehicles registered while the system limits how many are permitted on site at the same time according to the tenant's actual parking allocation.

### What happens when a tenant exceeds its parking quota?

The exact rule can be configured for the property. At Knight Frank Chatswood, the example supplied by Parkezi is that if a tenant with three bays already has three vehicles on site, a fourth can be notified to leave or be charged the casual parking rate.

### Does tenant quota management eliminate the need for swipe cards?

Where ANPR is used as the vehicle access method, physical parking credentials can be removed from the process. This also reduces the opportunity for cards or remotes to be shared between vehicles.

## Making Tenant Parking Easier to Manage

Multi-tenant parking becomes difficult when the access system cannot distinguish between a legitimate tenant vehicle and a tenant exceeding its allocated capacity.

Knight Frank and Liverpool demonstrate both sides of that problem. One shows how digital quotas can enforce allocations while still giving tenants flexibility. The other shows what can happen when shared physical credentials provide access without controlling how many vehicles are actually inside.

For commercial property managers, that is the real value of tenant quota management. It is not about creating more restrictions. It is about making existing lease allocations enforceable without creating another manual task for building management.

If tenant parking allocations are becoming difficult to administer or enforce at your property, [contact Parkezi](https://www.parkezi.com.au/contact) to discuss how ANPR-based quota management could be configured around the way your tenants actually use the building.

Tenant parking quotas
Tenant parking quotas
26 August 2026
A business leases three parking bays in a commercial building.
That sounds simple enough. Three bays should mean no more than three of that tenant's vehicles are parked on site at any one time.
But what happens if the business has six, eight or ten staff members who drive to work?
With a traditional swipe card or remote system, enforcing that three-bay allocation can become surprisingly difficult. Credentials get shared between staff, vehicles change from day to day and building managers can end up resolving disputes when one tenant's vehicles occupy spaces allocated to someone else.
This is not really an access problem. The vehicles may all belong to legitimate tenants.
It is a
quota management problem
.
For multi-tenant commercial properties, ANPR-based tenant parking management provides a different way to approach it. Instead of controlling which individual vehicles are permanently allowed to enter, the system can control how many vehicles each tenant is allowed to have on site at the same time.
Key Insight
Effective tenant parking management is not about limiting how many vehicles a business can register. It is about enforcing how many of those registered vehicles can occupy the car park at once.
Limits of traditional access
Swipe cards, remotes and fobs are designed primarily to answer one question: should the person holding this credential be allowed through the gate?
That works well for basic access control.
It becomes more difficult when the property needs to enforce commercial parking allocations.
Imagine a tenant leases three bays but has ten employees who occasionally drive to work. Giving the business only three swipe cards seems like an obvious solution.
The problem is that cards can be passed between staff.
One employee arrives, enters using the card and passes it to someone else. Before long, the business can have considerably more than three vehicles inside the car park.
The access system has technically done its job. Every entry used a valid credential.
But the parking allocation has failed.
This distinction is important for commercial property managers because it explains why simply upgrading a gate or issuing new cards does not necessarily solve tenant parking disputes.
Tenant Quotas Change What the System Controls
ANPR-based quota management approaches the problem differently.
Rather than making the physical access credential the centre of the system, vehicle registrations and tenant allocations are managed digitally.
A tenant can register multiple vehicles that may legitimately need parking access.
The quota then determines how many can be parked inside at the same time.
For example, a company leasing three bays might register:
Two directors' vehicles
Four employee vehicles
Two pool vehicles
There is no problem with having eight vehicles registered.
The important rule is that only three can occupy the tenant's allocated parking capacity at once.
When a fourth registered vehicle arrives, the system can identify that the tenant has already reached its quota and apply the appropriate rule.
That is fundamentally different from trying to control parking allocation by restricting the number of cards issued.
Knight Frank: tenant parking
Parkezi's Knight Frank Chatswood project provides a practical example.
The 120-bay property includes
50 leased bays and 70 casual bays
, with
18 tenant companies
using the building. Each tenant company has its own login.
Under the quota management system, tenants can whitelist as many vehicles as they need.
What they cannot do is occupy more bays than they have paid for.
If a tenant has three leased bays and four registered vehicles, any three of those vehicles can be on site.
When the fourth arrives, the system recognises that the tenant's allocation has already been reached. The additional vehicle can then be notified to leave or be charged the casual parking rate.
The property manager does not need to decide which three employees are permanently entitled to park.
There are no physical permits to distribute and no cards for the building manager to continually administer.
The tenant manages its own registered vehicles while the system enforces the actual parking allocation.
That flexibility is particularly useful in modern workplaces where the people coming into an office can change throughout the week.
Liverpool: quota enforcement
The Liverpool project demonstrates the same problem from the opposite direction.
The car park serves
more than 30 businesses
and previously relied on swipe cards.
Cards were being systematically shared. A business with three allocated bays could share one card among its staff and bring as many as ten vehicles into the facility.
Those additional vehicles did not simply exceed that tenant's allocation. They occupied parking intended for other businesses.
There was no quota system capable of enforcing how many vehicles each tenant had inside.
This is why parking allocation disputes can be difficult to solve manually.
From the driver's perspective, they may have legitimate access to the building. From the tenant's perspective, the employee may genuinely work there.
But neither fact means the business should be able to occupy more parking bays than it leases.
A parking management system needs to distinguish between
being an authorised user
and
having available parking allocation at that moment
.
Less building administration
Parking management often becomes a property manager's problem because the building manager sits between the tenant and the access system.
A new employee starts and needs parking.
Someone changes vehicles.
A registration needs updating.
A card is lost.
An employee leaves and access needs to be removed.
When all of those changes need to go through building management, a relatively simple parking facility can create an ongoing stream of administrative work.
Tenant-level access changes that relationship.
At Knight Frank, each of the 18 tenant companies has its own login.
That allows responsibility for managing authorised vehicles to sit closer to the business using the bays, while the overall parking rules remain controlled by the system.
For property managers, the benefit is not simply automation. It is removing routine parking administration that does not need to sit with the building management team.
Quota Management Also Makes Parking Fairer
Parking disputes are often difficult because enforcement can feel subjective.
One tenant may complain that another business is occupying too many spaces. Building management then has to investigate who owns the vehicles, whether they are authorised and which tenant should actually be using each bay.
A digitally enforced quota creates a clearer rule.
If a business leases five bays, its simultaneous parking allocation is five vehicles.
It can potentially register more vehicles to accommodate hybrid working, staff changes and different daily schedules, but the physical parking entitlement remains the same.
That makes the system more flexible for tenants without reducing fairness for everyone else in the building.
It also gives property managers a clearer basis for dealing with parking enquiries because access activity is recorded digitally rather than relying entirely on physical permits or observations.
Where Tenant Quota Management Makes Sense
Not every property needs sophisticated quota management.
A small building with a handful of permanent users and fixed allocated bays may be adequately managed with simpler access control.
Quota management becomes particularly useful where there are:
Multiple commercial tenants
Limited parking relative to staff numbers
Flexible or hybrid working arrangements
Regular changes in employee vehicles
Shared commercial parking areas
Ongoing disputes over parking allocations
Physical access credentials being shared
The more dynamic the tenant population becomes, the less practical it is to treat a swipe card as a reliable representation of a parking bay.
For properties facing those issues,
ANPR-enabled parking management
provides a way to connect vehicle access with actual parking entitlement.
Key Takeaways
Access permission and parking entitlement are two different things.
Swipe cards and remotes can be shared, making tenant parking quotas difficult to enforce.
ANPR allows tenants to register multiple legitimate vehicles without giving them unlimited parking capacity.
A tenant leasing three bays can register more than three vehicles while still being limited to three vehicles on site at once.
Knight Frank Chatswood uses tenant logins and quota management across 18 tenant companies.
At Liverpool, a lack of quota enforcement allowed businesses to significantly exceed their allocations by sharing swipe cards.
Tenant self-management can reduce routine administration for property managers.
Quota-based rules create clearer and fairer parking allocation across multi-tenant properties.

For a broader comparison of different access technologies, read
How to Choose the Right Parking Access Control System for Your Site
.
You can also explore Parkezi's
parking automation systems
to understand how ANPR, access control and cloud management work together.
If you are considering replacing an existing swipe-card or permit system, Parkezi's
parking system installation process
explains what is involved in planning an upgrade.
Questions answered

Yes. That is one of the advantages of quota-based management. A tenant can have multiple authorised vehicles registered while the system limits how many are permitted on site at the same time according to the tenant's actual parking allocation.

The exact rule can be configured for the property. At Knight Frank Chatswood, the example supplied by Parkezi is that if a tenant with three bays already has three vehicles on site, a fourth can be notified to leave or be charged the casual parking rate.
Are swipe cards still needed?
Where ANPR is used as the vehicle access method, physical parking credentials can be removed from the process. This also reduces the opportunity for cards or remotes to be shared between vehicles.
Making Tenant Parking Easier to Manage

Knight Frank and Liverpool demonstrate both sides of that problem. One shows how digital quotas can enforce allocations while still giving tenants flexibility. The other shows what can happen when shared physical credentials provide access without controlling how many vehicles are actually inside.
For commercial property managers, that is the real value of tenant quota management. It is not about creating more restrictions. It is about making existing lease allocations enforceable without creating another manual task for building management.
